Dzull a Latvian, and Kirecff, a Tartar.

WHEN YOU WEAR 


YOUR GLOVES... 


GEORGE MARGETTS, 


YEOVIL (Somerset). | 
fifty-seven-year-old glove 


maker, sits at home in his council house at Stoke, 


near Yeovil, wondering when he will work again. 


He 


knows where his work has gone—to Czecho-Slovakia and 
Italy, where it helps to build bombers. 


In Yeovil and the villages!Slovatian must work for 30s. a week. 


around, centre of England’s age- 
old glove industry, most of the 
world’s finest glove makers are 
working half time... Some, like 
George -Margetts, have not. 
worked for a year, 

But what hopes? Foreigners are 
making nearly half the gloves worn 


in .Britain. Czecho-Slovakia makes 
half of the gloves we import, 


When George Margetts works he’ 


Is paid 703, -week-but the Czecho~ 


Must George work for that? 
likely,” he says, 


If grey-haired Mrs, Margetts takes 
in some gloves to finish, as women 
do round here, she is paid) 30s, a 
week, Czecho-Slovaklan womenfolle 
are paid only 6s, a week, 


These Somerset’ men ore: no 
mackers, They regard It as an insult 
to go on the dole. They and their 
forefathers. have been used to sleady 
worl,

Charles Blake, president of the 
National Glovers’ Union, signs on at 
Yeovil Labour Exchange. 


* Rather thon have half of their 
number working all the time and 
half idle, the glovers agree to share 
the burden of unemployment that 
Czecho-Slovakia and Italy have put 
on them. 


The glovers lost to forelgners 
nearly £500,000 in wages last year 
up to October. : 


Forelmers sent 671,000 dozen 
pairs of leather gloves In that time— 
that is ene. pale ler every fifth person 
In England, Scotland und Wales— 
enough work to last 6,000 glove- 
makers a year, 


Czecho-Slovakia can sell gloves in 
thls country at 409, a dozen pairs, 
despite a 30 per cent, tariff, The 
nearest English type Is 48s. 


The Industry asked. the Import 
Dutles Advisory Committee last week 
for a duty of 35 ner cent., with a 
minimum duty of 15s, a dozen pairs. 

On the committee's decision rest 
the fate of George Margetts’s livell- 
hood and the (uture happiness of 
15,000 glove workers. --

‘Unknown’ Woman Buys 
Canute Relic 


R. WILLIAM RANDOLPH HEARST’S collection 
of silver from St. Donat’s, his castle in Wales, 
was sold in four hours for £41,882 at Christie’s, Lon- 


don, recently, 


‘ we 

The most exciting mo- 
ment of the four hours was 
when an ox’s horn, sparsely 
decorated with tarnished silver, 
was held up. At once there was 
a buzz of talk among’ the 300 
men present, : , 

The relic, is the Pusey Horn, piven 
to William’ Pewse *by Kinjt Canute, 
Danish ruler in England in 1017. 

Pewse, un officer of Canutc’s army, 
disgulscd himself as on shepherd and 
spied on the Saxons. He discovered 
a plan to ambush the | king, and 
saved his life. 

The horn was kept in the Pusey 
family unU] 1935, when it was sold 
to Mr. Hearst for £1,900. 

The recent bidding opened at 
£800, rose in £20 jumps to £1,000, 
and then by £50 bids to £1,900. 
This figure was reached in 
minutes. . 

At this price the horn was knecked 
down to Mr. J. Francis Mallet, who 
said he was acting for a woman who 
is kezping her name seeret. She is 
sivingt the relic to the Victoria and 
Albert Museum.

dress blazed up while she was warme

Nreguard which she thought.was per-

Sescribed by all her acquaintances as 
a wonderful mother.

by a spark coming through the guard.

Protest against the un-neutral actions 
of the secret.methods of the Presi-

acroplanes to France,

The science of colonisation fs one

it has.taken two principal forms. 
The enrliest colonists of whom we

the Greeks brought into Europe,

session. Hongkong, however, follow-

Ship Described 

The vessel “is‘the first of two 
orders placed with the bullders-and 
‘sa sister-ship tothe Glrnearn 
which rec ntly crrived in the Colony 
an her maiden voyage from England. 
She has on overall kemgth of 506 
feet, moulded beam of 66 fect and c 
depth “of 38 fect.. Three complete 


| steel decks are-ftted with poop, long 


bridge and forccastle ‘erccticns. A 
Anuble bottom !s fitted all fore and 
att for off fuct or water ballast, the 
peaks and one deep tank are avail- 
able for water bellast. 

There cre six cargo helds, one of 
which Js insulated for the carriage 
of frozen cargocs. Special attention 
has been given to eczrso gear, 28 
electric winches being fitted, operat- 
ing steel derricks 
lifts from 3 to 50 tons. The windinsa 
(: clectriesity driven, and the stecring 
gear Js of the electric hydraulic tyne. 

Accommodation for 32 first claus 
Passengsrs is arranged on promen- 
ade deck and js along luxuriqus 
Ines. Public rocms consist of dining 
coloon, lounge and amoke room. 
Officers and engineeriif‘nre accom- 
modated in houses amidthips and 
the crew berthed in ‘poop. *- 

The vessel ‘s equ'pned: to. comoly 
w'th the fatest official. regulations. 
The pronclling machinery consists of 
twin sects of six cylinder, two-stroke 
cycle, double-acting, marine diesel 
engines of the Burmelster and Wain 
type, each espable of giving a con- 
tinuous output of 9,000 S.LP, at 110 
RP.M. Special attention has been 
paid to the balancing of the engines 
to ensure a complete freedom from 
vibration throughout the . entire 
working range. : : : 

The auxiliary machinery through- 
out is of the most modern. elcctrical- 
ly, driven type; the electric power’ 
being supplicd by four. Diesel driven 
Renerating sets, ench giving a normal 
outout of 300 K.W. at an E.MLE. of 
220 volts D.C.

It’s easy with sheep and such. 
A sheep ls meant to be a sheep— 

. mutton.and overcoats. When o 
shecp has become a good fat sheep

trics to be anything more than a

racchorse and nothing but. When

in all directions, 1, 

Ono will suddenly get vp one

tine every day, summer and win-

people peep at them for a penny, 
It's a pity more don’t. I won't

aid one thing you did another.

HOSPITAL 


Financial Position 
Quericd By Hon. Sir 
-. Henry Pollock 


The question of. the financial posi. 
tlon of the Tung Wah and ollied 
Hospitals was raised in Council you~ 
terday by tho Hon. Sir Henry 
Pollock who, pursuant to notice, 
asked: “Has the Government ony 
statement to make as.to the financial 
position of the Tung Wah and allied 
Hospitals?” : 

The Colonial Scerctary: The qucs- 
tlon of Increased financlal assistance 
to the Tung Wah and assdelated hos- 
pituls has’ been under consideration 
for a considerable time. It came to 
a bend early in 1998 when the Tung 
Wah Committee approached 
Government with ‘a ‘request for a 
frant to cover the: deficit shown on 
the worlting for thd'year 1937, equal 
to opproxunately $160,000. As the 
result of discussions which followed, 
certaln important steps have been 
agreed for the improvement of the 
financial control of the hospitals, In 
particular it has been ogreed that: 

(a) The Committee shall prepore 
and adhere to a proper annual bud- 
get, and shall submit annual ac- 
counts audited by on auditor aop- 
proved by the Government. 

{b) The annual budget shall be 
approved by the Permanent Advisory 
Conunitice already established, 

(c) The medicab and charitable 
work of the hospitals shall be 
sepnrated, It is Intended that 
separate budgets shall be preparea 
for each of these lwo functions, and 
that the premises used for medical 
and hospital Purposes shull, os fur 
as possible, be separated from those 
used for the other great chorltable 
Works for which the Tung Wah Hos- 
pital organisation has: for long been 
well-known, namely the relief of dis- 
tress, however caused, the care of 
destitute and aged Persons, and the 
burial of the dead, 

Control by Committce 

(d) Alb masters relating to the 
medical administration of the oe 
the 
control of a Medical Committee ap- 
pointed by Hts Excellency the 
Governor. This committee -hus 
already been set up and its member- 
ship was announced in the Govern- 
ment Gazette of December 16,, 1938, 
It includes the Hanourable Director 
of Medical ‘Services or his deputy 
(Chairman), two members of the 
Tung Wah Hospital Advisory Com- 
mitice, three membtrs of vie Board 
of Directors of the Hospital Com- 
mittee, the Visiting Medical Oficer of 
hinese Hospitals and Dispensaries, 
and the three Medical Superinten- 
dents of the Tung ‘Wah, ‘tune wah 
Eastern and Kwong Wah Hospitals, 

(c) Chinese‘herbal treatment for 
medical in-patients i5-Yor. the Present 
to be provided in certain specified 
wards in cach of the hospitals to 
those who spontancously asi for it. 

(€) Future Investments of the 
Tung Wah funds will be in Bilt-edged 
sccuritics instead of in mortgayes and 


} property, 


Investigations of the accounts of

during 1937 and have fully satisfied

china To Compete 


“Av United Press message, published 
yesterday, stated that. China 
was one of 27 countrics which have 
signified their intention of participat- 
ing in the 1939 Davis Cup Competl- 
lion. This announcement has come 
ag a surprise to many people who 
had thought that owing to, the pre- 
sent trouble In the country China 
would not take part In this year’s 
tournament. China will. compete In 
the Europenn Zone, and will probe 
ably be represented: by Kho Sin-kle 
ond W. C. Choy, both of whom are 
still In Europe. While Kho has de- 
finttely madé great stridea towards 
reaching the top.of the tennis Orma- 
ment; it is pleasing to note that Choy 
has olso shawn considerable impreve- 
ment in his game. Last year he met 
with no Incenslderable success In the 
various provincial tourraments in 
England. Kho's greatest. . achieve- 
ment to date, of course, was h's vic- 
tory over “Bunny” Austin in the 
inal of the Bournemouth hardcourt 
championship last year. No better 
pair of players can China choose to 
represent her In this International 
competition. :

Continue

Burgos, Feb. 2. 

Insurgent..troops to-day occupicd 
Berga in the valley of Liobregat. 

Loyalist troops are reported to be 
retreating further on all fronts in the 
direction of the French frontler. 

A detailed statement regarding the 
capture of Berga says that the Insur- 
gents udvanced on Thursday morning 
in two columna on ‘the town, comes 
pletely surrounding the Loyalist 
starrison, which soon ceased. to offer 
further resistance, . 

The occupation of Ber#a brings-the 
Insurgent troops in this sector to 
within 35 miles of the French frontier 
at Puigcerda. 

It fs further announced that the 
Muestrazgo army corps has advanced 
a considerable distance beyond Vich 
and has captured the villagey of Seva, 
Tarndell, and Ruldeperas, thus cn- 
veloping .the Mont Seny mountain 
range in which.the Loyalist: artillery 
is entrenched,—Trans-Ocean,

Tanda Brings Large Hord 
.,.. From Australia 


Five calves born on board incrensed 
a stock consignment, brought by the 
Tonda from ‘Australia yesterday, to 
87, Fifty-two aro T, B. free cows 
being delivered: by Messra. Willlam 


A, Jones, Pty, Lid., Melbourne, to the, ; 


Hongkong Dairy Supply Company, 
whose herd, which now numbers | 
nearly 100, grazes on Jand ot Customs 
Pass Road. 

Tho shipment attracts attention to 
the fact that the Hongkong Dairy 
Supply Company, which was formed 
In 1937 to manufactur reconstituted 
milk, hag gone into original dairying. 
Its reconstituted product was first 
put on tho market list year. 

Good judgment attends the Tanda's 
delivery, for the 47 cows that have 
not yet calved will do so within a 
few weeks, ond the Dalry Company 
will have: a. fresh -herd -immedlately 
In-service. In any case Messrs. E. 
Mulholland ond J, Follett, Australian 
stockmen who attended the consign~ 
ment, were fortunate that only tive 
calves were born to add to the burden 
of looking after the 62 cows landed 
here and 10 landed at Manila. 

The Mantla consignment was dc- 
‘iivered to the Santa Mesa Dairy, 

which is also:changing over cntircly 


0! ¢o Australian stock, having found the 


breed more hardy than other cattle, 

Cows on board ship demand plenty 
of care. The Tanda'’s consignment 
was first watered and fed each day 
nt 4a.m., fed ngain at midday, water- 
ed and fed once more at 3.30 p.m., 
und then given as much lucerne hay 
us they might wish to toy with during 
the night. Keeping to. this pro- 
sramme, they note thelr way through 
306 bags of chaff, 92 bags of bran, 
and about 11 tons of lucerne by the 
time they reached Hongkong. They 
showed the benefit of ft, arriving in 
thriving condition. 


Big Shipment In August 


- Sixteen were located In stalls on 
the deck, the remainder being on the 
shelter deck. The Tandn's shelter 
deck accommodation makes her a 
popular ship for stock deliveries. Its 


trip when she will carry 150 cattle 
and horses, the horses being for the 
Hnogkong Jockcy Club. 

Mr. W. A. Jones arrived by the 
Tanda to deliver the cattle.’ He will 
go with the Tanda to Shanghal, re- 
turning before the ship to Hongkong 
and Manila, and joining her at the 
latter-place for the return trip to 
Australia. 


COW CAUSES PANIC 
A Uvely  eptsode was associated 
with the. unloading of 10 Australian 
cows from the Tanda at Manila on 
her way to Hongkong. Nine of the} 
cows went quictly, but the other took 
fright at the brightly colourcd cloth~ 
ing of the clustering Filipinos and 
bolted down the wharf. 
) - The seene immediately became one 
of panic., There was o wild scramble 
Sof shouting Filipinos, one’ youth 
leaping on to a bag of fodder less 
than an. inch ahead of the animal's 
plunging horns. . 
At the end of the wharf the cow 
was confronted by a caromata drawn 
by Sts small Fillpino pony. The terri- 
fied animal charged the vehicle, and 
when it drew back the pony was 
lying ‘on the ground, dying. The 
cow raced back along the wharf, 
paused at the cdge, and then, almost 
deliberately, dived “into: the. water.

who had come up in charge of the

TAIPAN 


A HAPPY CEREMONY took 
place at noon to-day uboard the 
Italian. liner Conte. Verde when 
Mr. E. Cock, M.B.E., A.M.LC.E., 
M.LN.A,, head of the Hongkong 
& Whampon Dock Co., received 
-the. insignia of the Order of 
Knight Commander of the Crown 
of Italy. 


The decoration was ‘bestowed by 
the. Marqulse G. Pagano di Melilto, 
Italian Consul General {n’ Hongkong, 
in the presence of a large fathering. 

Mr. G. B. Scarpa, agent for the 
Lloyd Triestino Company in Hong- 
kong, officers of the ship, and friends 
of Mr, Cock were present.

tude for Mr. Cock’s services In 
securing tho re-floating and repair- 
ing of the Conte Verde In good 
time when she was stranded outside 
Lyeemun Pass in the typhoon of 
September 2, 2037,0 °°

SOVIET SEVERS RELATIONS 


WITH HUNGARIANS 


® 


ar 


on 


reer 


\COUNTRY,” MOSCOW ALLEGES | 


: a & ieee MOSCOW; FEB. 2.. 
MAXIM LITVINOV, THE SOVIET FOREIGN MINISTER, HAS 


* INFORMED THE HUNGARIAN MINISTER THAT THE SOVIET 
GOVERNMENT HAS DECIDED TO SEVER DIPLOMATIC RELATIONS 


WITH HUNGARY. 


The decision has been taken owing to German pressure on Hungarian 
affairs, and because of Hungary's adhesion to the anti-Comintorn Pact. 
M. Litvinov’ informed the Hungarian Minister that the Soviet 
Legation at Budapest would be closed, and. the Sovie;. Government 
expected the closure of the Hungarian. mission in Moscow. 7 
According to the official agency, the decision of, thé Soviet is 
connected with the fact that as a result of the Munich agreement, 


Hungary has lately been subjected to strong 


States, and the “policy of the Hungarian 
Government shows that it easily yielded to this 
pressure and has lost its independence to a 
considerable degree.” 


AGGRESSIVE AIMS 


It is pointed out that the decision of the Hungarian 
Government to join the anti-Comintern Pact cannot he 
justified by the interests of the Hungarian State itself, 
which by no means coincide with the aggressive aims 
pursued under the guise of this pact by its participants, 
and in the first place by Japan. : : 

Consequently, the-decision has been forced upon the 
Hungarian Government from outside. 


Soviet circles explain that distinction is made between 
other signatories of the pact and Hungary because the 
other signatorics are still independent States. It is declared 
that “Hungary is now a vassal of the Rome-Berlin axis, and 

- docs not deserve full diplomatic status.”—Reuter, : 


A_GREAT SURPRISE. 


The decision of the Soviet 
Government - to break: off - diplo- 
matic relations with Hungary 
has ealled forth the following

CRASH 


Shot Down By 
Arab Irregulars 


JERUSALEM, Feb. 2. 

A BRITISH AEROPLANE 
was shot.down by Arab irregu- 
jars while cruising over the 
village of Deir Ballut, at a low 
altitude to-day, the British pilot 
being killed in the crash. 

The machine, with others, had 
been ordered to assist searching 


partics at work In the Samaria dis- 
trict. 


Numerous other minor Incidents 
which occurred to-day indicate that 
Arab resistance has not diminished. 

A train was wrecked on the Jaffa- 
Jerusalem route as a result of 
sabotage, although the extent of the 
damage is not: yet known. 

A bomb was tossed into the office 
of a Jewish taxicab company In 
Jerusalem, the building being prac-

THE RECENT Anglo-German 
naval conference in Berlin, in 
the colrse of which Germany 
announced. her. intention of in- 
creasing her submarine tonnage 
up to the limit provided for in 
the Anglo-German naval agree 
ment, was the subject of the fol- 
lowing announcement made Ly 
the semi-official German News 
Agency this evening: 

“The German Government inform- 
ed His Britannic Majesty’s Govern- 
ment last December of its intention 
of excroising the rights accorded to 
it through the Anglo-German naval 
agreement of June 18, 1934, and July 
17, 10937. 

“A frlendly conference was held 
in Berlin on December 30, concerning 
certain questions that arose from the 
exercising of these rights. 

“The German Government agreed 
at that tlme to provide the British 
Government with a written state- 
ment. 


TO BEGIN IMMEDIATELY 


“On January 18 of this year the 
German Government handed to the 
British Government a written state- 
ment regarding the intentions os re- 
vealed at the aforementioned confer- 
ence. = - : 
“Germany will, accordingly, -in- 
crease her submarine tonnage to 
parity with that of the British Em- 
pire, the beginning to be made in 
1939, and construction to be con- 
(Inued untll the treaty Imit Is 
achleved, ; 

“Germany will, furthermore, ‘arm 
two !0,000-ton crulsers in such. a 
manner that they will be advanced 
from crulsers of sub-class’ B to 
erulsers of sub-class A. In doing so, 
Germany is exercising the right 
accorded to her in the agreement."

pledged American support for de-

the Totalltarians,

papers and public.speukers are un- 
animous In their expreesion of 
graufication, “7

that President Roosevelt is merely 
expressing the fact that the ideals 
for which the United States stands 
will be. won or lost; not on the 
Mississippi, but on the Rhine, 

The Labour “Dally Herald” believes 
that war in Europe is a great deal 
less Hkvly now that the Totalitarians 
know where America stands. It 
warns, however, that no more should 
be rend into the reports of Mr. 
Roosevelt's ndvice than was given.

Eulogistic Telegram 
To Franco 


BURGOS, Feb, 2. 
EX-KING ALFONSO of 
Spain, and his son, Don Juan, to- 
day exchanged telegrams with 
General Franco on the occasion 


of the fall of Barcelona. 

The message from King Alfonso 
reads as follows: “My enthusiastic 
and heartfelt congratulations, also to 
the whole glorious army Iced by Your 
ExecHency, and my. gratitude as a 
Spaniard, together with the assurance 
of my devotion and of my confidence 
In the re-awakening of our country.” 

Gencral Franco replied: ‘Most 
grateful for your congratulations and 
Your tribute to the. army, as well os 
for the ussurance of your devotion 
and of your confidence. I send sin- 
cere and hearty grectings.” 

It is stated that the telegram ecx- 
changed between Don Juan-nnd Gen- 
eral Franco were couched in equally 
cordial terms.—Trans-Ocean.

treasures) are .usually stored, He

The King May Radio ~Instructions from 
U.S.A. to His Council of Sta 


COUNCIL of State will be 
appointed to act for the King 
while he and the Queen are on thelr 
Canadian and American tour in June. 
The Councillors will be the Duke 
of Gloucester, the Duke of Kent, the 
Princess Royal, and Princess Arthur 
of Connaught, 

If the King had been travelling 
alone the Queen would have been the 
first Councillor, 

By letters patent under the Great 
Seal the King will delegate to the 
Councillors of State certain of the 
Royo! functions, Including preroga~ 
tive and statutory powers. They will 
also be empowered to receive any 
homujte to be done to the King. 

The functions will be specified jh 
the Ietters patent, but they will not 
include power to dissolve Parliament, 
This remains In the personal control 
of the King. 

Provision Is made, however, su 
that [f the Prime Minister should 
ask for a Goneral Election in the 
King’s absence the request can be 
conveyed to the King, who can tele- 
raph instructions to the Councillors. 

This opens up the possibillly, remote 
though tt is, of the King dissolving 
Parliament in London by an edict 
issued from the seat of the American 
Government tn, Washington.

Instinct Told Her 

6OUMJE were supposed to be married 

on October 5, but I had a 
sort of presentiment,”" said Miss 
Florence Flint recently at Marylebone 
Pollce Court when she alleged that 
thirty-nine-year-old) Walter Evans 
took her savings of more than £240 
and disappeared on the eve of their 
wedding in order to marry somsbody 
else, 

Evans, it was said, took a room at 
Miss Flint's houss in Pimlico-road. 
S.W,, and after a few days proposed 
to her und was accepted, 

He suggested that they should take 
aosmall restaurant together. ‘She 
withdrew her savings of more than 
£300 and handed the money to 
Evans to put into a joint account. 

Out of the meney Evans bought 
her ‘ny wedding ring and himself a 
watch, On October 4 he dis- 
appeared, 


Evans pleaded = guilty, and 9 was 


ing, her hands by the bedroom fire, 

Mrs. May Gilson, her mother, sald 
the grate was fitted with a big heavy 
fireguard which she thought was per- 
fectly safe. . 

Police - Constable Pearce, the 
coroner's officer, said the fireguard 
was of the type used In schools and 
he considered it most effective. Mrs. 
Gilson, who had ten children,. was 
described by all her acquaintances as 
a wonderful mother. 

The coroner. Mr. T. Ottoway, sald 
the tragedy might have been caused 
by a spark coming through the guard, 
He felt very sorry for Mrs. Gilson, 
who was obviously a mother who had 
taken great care of her. children.

SOUTH AFRICA. . 


NATIVE WELFARE 
REFORMS 


Cape Town, 

Far-reaching plans are under con- 
sideration for widening the sphere of 
activities of the Department of 
Native Affairs. It is proposed to 
centralise the native welfare activi- 
tes at present distributed among 
various Government departments 
Under the one department, ‘i 


Mr. D. L. Smit, Secretary for 
Native Affalrs, told the Native Re- 
presentative Council that the Govern- 
ment intended ‘to introduce legisla- 
tion abolishing. the issuing by traders 
of credit tokens to native mine 
workers, : 


He described as “a public scandal” 
the practice whereby traders were 
allowed to Ict natives mortgage their 
entire wages. i

Wang Leaving 
Hanoi For 
European Tour 


It igs confirmed from usually well- 
informed Chinese circles that Wang 
Ching-wei, former vice-president of 
the Kuomintang who was expelled 
from the party in consequence of his 
peace move at the end of last year, 
will shortly Ieave Hanoi for Europe. 

The passport for which Wang re- 
cently applied to the Ministry of 
Forelgn Affairs, has.already been re 
lensed from Chungking, it is learned. 

It is believed that he will sail 
directly to France from Hano!, where 
he has been staying since his secret 
depariure from Chungking on Decem- 
Der 18 last year. He will be accom- 
panied by his wife and Tsang Chung- 
ming. 

With confirmation of Wang's in- 
tention to leave for Europe, the 
Japanese reports to the elfect that the 
former Kuomintang leader will join 
the puppet government in’ Nanking! 
are dispelled.

ELEGRAPH 


British Envoy 

Welcomed «| 
Visit To Tientsin 
Excites Chinese — 


| CHUNGKING, Feb. 3. 

SIR ARCHIBALD KERR 
CLARK KERR, the British Am- 
bassador to China, ‘has been 
accorded a hearty welcome by 
the Chinese people in Tientsin 
since his arrival there. fe 

During the past two days, accord- 


dng to a Tientsin message received | 


here, a large number of Ictters have 
been sent to the Brillsh envoy by the 
Chinese people, paying respects and 
declaring that they are loyal to the 
National’ Government and will sap- 
port the present war of resistance to 
the bitter end, : 
-Sir-Archibald bad--an - interview 
with the British Consul-Gencral, Mr. 
J. B, Affleck, on Jan. 1, during which 
the latter reported the situation of 
the British Concession in Ticntsin. 

The British Envoy is expected to 
visit the Race Course, where he will 
obtain a view of Hsin Hsing Road, a 
new_road built by the Japanese to 
encircle the British Cancession. 

-He will - proceed to-morrow to 
Peiping where he will stay for five 
days. Then he will go to Tangshan 
to inspect the Kailan Mining Ad- 
ministration. Afterwards, he will 


; return to Shanghai via Chinwangtav. 


Since the arrival of the British 
Envoy in Tientsin, the Japanese there 
linve tightened their restrictive mea- 
sures ngainst the British and French 
Concessions. : : 

They have closed the East Station 
of the Peiping-Linoning Railway, 
which Is adjacent to the two foreign 
areas, and all passengers travelling: 
on this rallway have to use the Hopcl 
New Statlon.—Central News.

Mariner Dies

The death occurred in Kowloon 

Hospital thls morning of Captain 
Thon Balfour Robertson, formerly 
skipper of the Kaiapof, which sank off 
the Fukien coast Inst week. 
- Captain Robertson, who has been 
an employee of Williamsons ‘for the 
last elght years, was 55 years of age. 
He had been Hl for about. a week. 
ile was well-known up and down the 
China coast, and was regarded as an 
exemplary, officer. ? : 

Ills leaves’ a widow, who is in 
Tasmania. : 

The funeral will take place this 
afternoon, entcring the Stubbs Rond 
gate at 5.30 p.m. 


Lost Wallet, So 


Jumped into Harbour) 


Apparently upset at the loss of his 
wallet contalning $53, it is alleged 
that Chung Tai-chung, of Kowloon 
City. yesterday left the tram on which: 
he wus travelling between Des Vocux 
Road Central and Leighton Hill, and 
threw himself into ‘the harbour-in an 
attempt to commit suicide. 

He was rescued. 

Chung was on his way to the poilce 
station to report his loss, The. wal- 
let was apparently stolen from him 
while he was on the tram.

REPUBLICANS ACT 
: Washington, Feb. 2. 

Nine Republican members of the 
House of Representatives Foreign 
Affairs’ Committee to-day issued a 
statement in which.they “deplore and 
protest against the un-neutral actions 
of the secret methods of the Presi- 
dent” in connection with the sale of 
aeroplanes to France. ~ 
-. There .ig.no -objection against the 
snle of acroplanes to a nation to 
which the Unite}! States Is diploma- 
tleally related, but we insist that 
such un-neutral acts entangle us in 
foreign confilcts, and endanger the 
peace of America.” 

They appealed to the President to 
present the facts to the American 
people who “irrespective of party, ure 
teing committed to a war programme 
through secret diplor.acy.” 

The statement urged the President 
to uphold traditional forelyn policy 
of neutrality, non-Intervention, and 
neace.—Reuter,

STIRRING 
DEFENCE - 
‘OF JEWS: 


Hitler Impeached 
. In World Appeal 


GENEVA, Feb. 2. 

THE TEXT OF A state- 
ment issued by the head- 
quarters of the World Jew 
Congress was issued to- 
night, and is as follows: 

“In view of the outrageous 
attacks against the world ‘of 
Jewry made by tho Chancellor 
of the Reich in his last speech, 
it is our duty to make it known 
ta the governments and public 
opinion in civilised nations, the 
profound indignation felt by 
millions of Jews represented by 
the World Jew Congress. 


THREAT OF EXTERMINATION 


“The head of the German Govern- 
ment not only insults our unfortunate 
Jews [In Germany, whose lives are 
made intolerable by high regime, 
who are prevented from praying in 
their synagogues“or even from seck- 
ing an asylum elsewhere; Is not con 
tent to ridicule nations who are ably 
endeavouring to help the victims of 
Nazi persecution; who does not con- 
fine himself to threatening millions 
of Jews with complete extermination, 
but is trying to stir up all the nations 
of the world against them. 


“The Chancellor of. the Reich even 
affirms that it is not Germany which 
threatens the peace of the world, but 
that it is the Jews who want war. 


‘Wo repudiate firmly, and with 
horror, this ou! ‘cous accusation, 
coming from man whoso notorious 
book ‘Mein Kampf vindicatcs tho 
necessity of war, which alone, ne- 
cording to him, is capable of 
realising the nims of his policy. 


SEPTEMBER CRISIS 


"We desire to reeall to the world 
that the countries which, in Septem- 


{ber last, in their lust for conquest, 


brought the world to within an ace of 
a most dreadful catastrophe, and 
who, by so-called ‘natunal'. nsptra- 
Nons, recently created a new and ex- 
tremely dangerous crisis. 


“"It is in just those countries where 
the Jews exert no influence whatever, 
and where the Jews do not even 
Possess clementary rights, ~ 


“If tho Jows havo been climinat- 
ed from national life, this ty pre- 
elsely because their religion, thelr 
mentality, their sentiments and | 
thelr’ tradilions are opposed to 
every enterprise of aggression and! 
violence, and to every act contrary 
t tho Inws of humanity 
and solidarity between men."— 
United Press, :

KING. ACTS | 
IN CRISIS 
IN BELGIUM 


BRUSSELS, Feb. 2. 

KING LEOPOLD'S interven- 
tion in certain questions of home 
policy in the course of the 
Cabinet Council meeting held 
here to-day was stressed in a 
communique issued. later by 
Premier Spank. : 


The communique’states that the 
King dwelt on the necessity firstly, 
of precision in matters ‘of cconamic 
policy, secondly, of adaption of a 
budget to the financial resources of 
the country, and thirdly, of the 
development of social Iegistiation, 


The King also discussed the ques- 
tion of constitutional guarantees, and 
emphasised the principle of scpara- 
tion of executive and legislative 
powers, and insisted that administrao- 
tive reforms’ should be carried out 
without further delay. 


‘Informed quarters add that the 
king expressed the carnest desire | 
that the constantly increasing fric- 
tion between the parties in Parlla- 
ment should be’ diminished © In’ ‘the 
interests of the nution.--Trans-Occan, 


TURBULENT SESSION 


An attack on Premier Spal, says 
u “Trans-Ocean” message occurred 
after a turbulent session in the Cham- 
ber of Deputies, during which 
Premier Spnak was violently attack~ 
ed by the opposition during a debate 
on a plan submitted by-the Walloon 
Socialist, M, Truffaut, recommending 
the conversion of Belgium into a 
Sederal State, and the establishment 
of administrations for the Flemish 
and Walloon regions. 

The proicet was supported by the 
Flemish Nationalists, and finally re- 
Jected by 111 votes to 62, ‘after 
violent scenes. 

Demonstrators ossalicd M. Spaak, 
when he returned home to confer with 
the presidents of the Croix De Feu 
and. the Belgian ex-servicemen’s 


| 


organisations, had previously attempt. 
ed to force their way into the 
Government quarter in the central 
part of the city, which, however, they 
led by strong cordons of 
Ppollee. Oo eo : 


The demonstrators showed by thelr 
cries that they included Natlonalists 
as well’ a3 Left-Wing extremists and+ 
others,’ as some shouted “Long Live 
King Albert,” and others “Long Live 
The Republic.” 

Dispersal of the demonstrators led 
to clashes during which several were

Ma Chan-shan To 
Fight Japanese 


CHUNGKING, Feb, 2. 

A RELIABLE MILITARY 
source stated to-day that General 
Ma Chan-shan, who, shortly 
after the Mukden Incident in 
1931 resisted the Japanese 
advance toward North Man- 
churia at Ronni River, is going 
back to Manchuria to fight the 
Japanese. ia 

This informant said’ that General 
Moa Chan-shan was at present 
commander of a “stiff advance army", 
which is fighting. the Japanese in 
western Suljyuan, and ‘his men are 
Manchartan native cavalry. . 

It ls remembered that General Ma, 
after his Ronni: battles surrendered 
to the Japanese outwardly, and the 
Japanese appointed him Min'ster of 
War in Manchukuo, ‘ , 


TRICKED JAPANESE 


Gencral Ma, after swallowing up 
a huge sum of Japanese money, and 
huge quantitics of Japanese cquip- 
ment, turned his guns against the 
Japanese in 19392 and fought a 
gueriNla war in North China until he 
Was hard-pressed over the Man. 
churian border, when he = entered 
Soviet territory. | Eres, 

He was sent to Central Asia and 
his men were sent back to China 
through Sinkiang, but he himself, 
with, other commanders of the 
Manchuria guerillas jrcturned. to 
Chiha after a trip throuth Europe. 

Since hfs return in 1033, General 
Ma Chan-shan has been honoured by 
being given a position as a member 
of the National Military Affairs 
Council, of which © Generalissimo 
Chiang Kaleshek Is the President. 

In the meantime, he never gave up 
his efforts to fight the Japanese, and 
his cavalry forces, under the title of 
a “sth! Advance Army” are fighting 
the Japanese in western Suiyuan, 
checking the threatened Japanese 
advance from Paotow to Ningshiao. 
—United Press,

pledged American support to

with the Totalitarian countries

The freedom of the subject 
is the very corner-stone of de- 
mocratic principles, and any 
movement which threatens to 
challenge that freedom must be 
vigorously opposed. °

the others may

BY WILL SCOTT 


N all the muddle of to-day 
few people seem to be quite 
clear as to where mankind 
ts supposed to be heading, 


although plenty will tell you. 


that mankind is heading, and 
‘pretty rapidly. It would be a 
bit easter if we knew what man- 
kind {s jor. Then we might 
know {f it's heading right or 
wrong. 


It’s easy with sheep and such, 
A sheep is meant to be a sheep— 
mutton and overcoats. When oa 
sheep has become a good fat sheep 
{t's got as far as It can go. It never 
tries to be anything more than o 
good fat shecp, 

A racchorse 1s meant ‘to be o 
racehorse and nothing but. When 
it has won the Derby it's done all 
you can expect of it, But with 
men you never know. They go off 
in nil directions, 

One will suddenly get up one 
morning and design St. Paul's 
Cathedral, Another will discover 
the South Pole. A third will add 
up another man’s figures and get 
bread and butter for doing it. A 
fourth wlll dive into the Serpen- 
tine every day, summer and win- 
ter. 

Some, even, sit in barrels and Ict 
people peep at them for a penny. 
It's a plty more don’t. I won't 
mention any names. In the old 
days the iden was to be differ- 
ent, You developed yourself, 
or tried to. If your neighbour 
did one thing you did another, 
That was tho root of the whole

| HIGHWAY .| TUNG WAH 


HOSPITAL 


Financial -Position 
| ° Queried By Hon. Sir 
Henry Pollock 


The question of the Anancial posi. 


tion of the Tung Wah and © allied 
Hospltals was raised In Council yes- 
terday by the Hon, Sir Henry 
Pollock who, pursuant to notice, 
anked: “Has the Government any 
statement to muke as to the financial 
positlon of the Tung Wah and allled 
Hospitals?” . 

The Colonial Sccretary: The ques- 
tlon of increased Anancint assistance 
to the Tung Wal: and associated hos- 
pltals has been“ under consideration 
for a considerable time. It came to 
a bead carly in 1938 when the Tung 
Wah Committee approached the 
Government ‘with o request for o 
frant to cover the deficit shown on 
the working for the year-1937, equal 
to approxlnately $150,000. As the 
result of discussions Which followed, 
certain Important steps have been 
agreed for the Jmprovement: of the 
financin) control of the hospitals. In 
particular {{ has been agreed that: 

(a) The Committee shall prepare 
and adhere to a proper annual bud- 
get, and shall submit) annual | ace 
counts audited by an auditor ap- 
proved by the Government, 

(b) The annunl budget shall be 
approved by the Permanent Advisory 
Committee already established, 

(ec) The medical and charitable 
work of the hospitals shall be 
separated, It is intended that 
Separate budgets shall be prepared 
for each of these two functions, and 
that the premises used for medical 
and hospital purposes shall, as far 
és possible, be separated from those 
used for the other great. charitable 
works for which the Tung Wah Hog- 
pital organisation has for Jong been 
well-lone yamely the relief of dis- 

z however caused, the care of 
tute and aped persons, and tho 
Vof the dead. 
Control by Committee 

(di) AN mutters relating 
medical administration of the 
pitals will in fugre be under 
controt of a Medical Committee 
pointed by ‘His Excellency the 
Governor, This — committee has 
already been set up and its member- 
ship wos announced in the Govern- 
ment Gazette of December 16, 1938. 
It includes. the Honourable Director 
of Medical Se cs or his deputy 
(Chairman), two members of the 
Tuny Wah Hospital Advisory Com- 
mittec, three members of the Board 
of Directors of the Hospital Com- 
mittee, the Visiting Medical Officer of 
Chinese Hospitals dnd Dispensuries, 
and the three Medical’ Superinten- 
dents of the Tung Wah, Tung Wah 
Eastern,and Kwong Wah Hospitals, 

(c) Chinese herbal treatment for 
medical in-patients is for the present 
to be provided in certaln specified 
wards in each of thé hospitals to 
those who spontaneously ask for it. 

(f) Future investments of the 
Tung Wah funds will be in uilt-edged 
securities Instead of in mortgares and 
property. 

Investigations of the accounts of 
the hospitals have already been made 
during 1937 and have fully satisfied’ 
the Government of the aecuracy of 
the accounts os submitted’ and of the 
satisfactory nature of the system of 
accounting, subject to certaln minor. 
improvements recommended by the 
Qecountants who made the Investiga- 

ons, 


to the, 
Hos- 
the 
ap- 


2 Increased “Expenditure: ° 

A supplementary vote for the grant 
of an additionnl sum of $150,000, the 
approximate deflelt for the year 1937, 
is being submitted at the mecting of 
the Financa Committee which follows 
immediately the meeting of this 
Council, This deficit was due, (a) to 
increased expenditure due to 
attempts to improve the standard of 
treatment; (b) to the increased num- 
ber of patients partly os a result of 
the present hostilities; (¢) to re- 
duced income from investments: and 
(d) to the partial drying up of the 
fluw of subscriptions in favour partly 
of the claims of war charities. 

Under these arrangements =the 
Government has every confidence 
that the valuable work done in the 
past for the Chinese community of 
the Colony by the Tung Wah and 
associated Hospitals will not -only 
continue but will increase in value, 
and that the existing co-operation 
between the Directors and the Gov- 
ernment will be strengthened without 
any chonge in the fundamental 
characteristic of voluntary institu. 
tions supported in the “main by 
private contribution. It is indeed 
hoped that the assurance of efficient 
administratlon which ts afforded by 
the steps now taken will’ encourage 
the charitably-minded members of 
the community to continue and in- 
crease their support of this worthy 
foundation. ‘

Manila Preparing For 
_Interport With H.K. 


een eer players have been 
picked by the Manila Football 
League to undergo training for the 
purpose of selecting a side to repre- 
sent the Philippines in the = first 
Mantla-Hongkong Interport to be 
played In .the Colony during the 
Chinese New Year holidays. The 
names of the players were announced 
Inst week following a mecting of 
officials of the League. Of the 28 
selected, four are goal-keepers, four 
are full backs, seven half-backs and 
13 are . forwards. Following — the 
series of try-outs which have been 


arranged, a team of 16 players will 
be chosen to make the trip to Hong- 
Kong. Coach Dionisio Calvo of the 
La Salle College’ has been selected 
by the oMcials of the Manila Ama-= 
teur Football League to head the 
delegation. An experlenced soccer 
conch, it is confidently expected 
that Mr. Calvo will be able to get 
the best out of his men. The team 
will leave Manila on February 14 
and is expected to return on Febru- 
ary 27 by the ss. Biancamano. On 
the return trip, the Manila ployers 
will go to Macao to play one game 
there. To help Conch Caivo In the 
finat selection of the team, a spectal 
committee was chosen, made up of 
Fr. Sergio Martinez of San Beda, Fr. 
Honorlo Munoz of Letron and GA, 
Reedyak of the Yco Athletic Club. 
Honglsong players will be Interested 
to learn that among the 28 men 
selected to undergo training Is Alex 
Botsserie, the former Shanghai Inter- 
porter who is now resident in 
Manila, An extremely clever inside 
forward, Bolsserie has a very good 
chance of being chosen to make the 
trip.

On Wednesday 


Over a hundred members turned up 
for the Spoon and Practice Shoot last 
Wednesday, and some high 
was registered, Conditions were 
good at the start, but the fresh and 
constant wind became a series of 
variable alr. puffs later on, which 
caused some trouble‘ omongst the 
“dead-cyes,”

on return from Camp, and Licut 
Dawson took the Nett S.R.(b) Spoon 
with a 98 Including a possible at 500 
yards, The 4th Submarine Flotilla

Ship’s Company, cight a-side. All 
the officers fred with the S.R.(a), 
and four Ship's Company with 
S.R.(b), and four S.R.(0). The in- 
triente handicaps were worked out 
and the Ship's Company defented the 
OMcers. by 767,03 to 698 points!

the Ship's Company.

C. B. FRY’S 
RECORD 
EQUALLED 


Rain Kept Him Waiting 
For Two Days 


London, Jan. 19, 
Don Bradman, after rain had kept 
him waiting for two days, completed 
his century for South Australia 
against New South Wales on 18th 
January, and equalled C. B. Fry's 
first-clnsa cricket record by scoring 

centuries in six successive innings. 


Bradman, 22 not out on Saturday, 
carried his score to 135 not out, and 
then declared South Australia's Inn- 
ings closed nt 349 for four, giving 
them a first innings lead of 103. 


In their second innings New South 
Wales, scored 156 for five wickets, 
and the match was left drawn. 


Since he returned from the tour 
in England, which ended for him 
with a foot Injury in the last Test, 
Bradman has played only ‘six first~ 


class Innings. In these he has scor-! 


ed 914 runs for an average of 182.8,

THER offenders 
against the laws of 
health are office workers who 
snatch at meals. There should 
be an inquiry -into the hours of 
oMce workers like the recent 
servants, So many 
women prefer -to- havea snack 
at their desk xather than make 
the effort to get out for Junch. 
Some save the valuable hour by 
cating hastily at work -and 
spending the rest of the time 
‘ These’ will not 
suffer so badly because at least 
they get fresh air and mental 
relaxation, 


We need a revival of the art 
of living to fit present day rush 
and strain. It should be made 
illegal for anyone to read a 
business Ietter or to continue 
working whilst cating. 


Lectures on physiology should 
be popularised _go that tired 
stomachs, torpid livers and 
overworked gall-bladders would 
be given a chance to function 
properly because their owners 
understood how they worked. 


Light conversation or any 
amusing book may be peimitted 
at an office lunch. But the 
very atmosphere of work does 
not stimulate .the bodily pro- 
cesses like a walk in the fresh 
air or the pleasant aroma from 
a restaurant. The sight and 
smell of food bring jncreased 
saliva to our mouths which acts 
reflexly by causing an outpour- 
ing of gastric juices. That is 
just the right moment to sit 
down to food, listen to music, 
or gaze upon others and make 
idle chatter.

ing work before beginning to 
eat. If wo must eat alone a 
light book ora picture pape. 
will distract our thoughts and 
heip us to eat slowly,

with chopped hard-boiled egs, and 
parsley on top.

locs, :

and sprinkle well with white bread- 
crumbs,

Unned salmon, the fat in which the 
onions were fried, and a tablespoon 
of watcr. Pour this over the mix- 
ture, and bake In a moderate hot 
ovén 20 minutes, 


Sabnon Loaf

salmon. Mix in with it 2 oz. bread-

beaten, 1 gill milk, and seasonings of 
pepper and salt.

minutes. Turn out, and garnish with 
Pour’ 4% pint white 
sauce round.

KULING 


‘Japanese Give Orders 
To Forcignors 


Shanghai, Feb, 2. | 
A Japanese spokesman announced 
to-day that the Japanese authorities 
yesterday natifed the forelgn con- 
sular authoritles to evacuate all their 
nationals © from Kullng _ before 
February 10, after which date the 
Japanese army would take absolutely 
no responsibility for the safety of 
foreign lives and property at Kuling. 
The notice stated that foreigners 
must evacuate to the Japanese lines 
in a single group at o sct time and 
carry national flags. 


It was indicated that the Japanese 
army would bomb the area, regard- 
less of Whether Chinese guerillas held 
foreigners os hostages. This was 
Chungking's responsibility. Foreign 
property was unsafe owing to guerll- 
ln operations in the vicinity, ; 

The foreigners at. Kullng comprise 
37 Britons, 12 Americans, nine Ger- 
mans and seven of other nationall- 
tles, Many are elderly and have 
been ‘bed-ridden 
United Press, 


for many years —

COW CAUSES - PANIC 

A lively episode was assoclated 
with the unloading of 10 Australian 
cows from the Tanda at Manila on 
her way to Hongkong. Nine of the 
cows went quictly, but the other tool 
fright at the brightly coloured cloth- 
ing of the clustering Filipinos ond 
bolted down the wharf. 

The scene immediately became one 
pf panic. There was o wild scramble 
one youth 
lenping on to a bag of fodder less 


Jthan on inch ahead of the animal's. 


plunging horns. 

At the end of the wharf the cow 
was.confranted by a caromata drawn 
by its small Filipino pony. The terrl~ 
fied animal charged the vehicle, and 
when it drew back the pony was 
lying on the ground, dying. The 
cow raced bacie along the wharf, 
puused ot the edge, and then, almost 
deliberately, dived- Into -the’ -water... . 

One of the Australian stockmen 
who had como up in charge of the 
eattle promptly dived in after the 
cow. He pursued her‘ under the 
whart and out the. other side, where 
he caught her, He held ‘her there 
until a crane could Jower o net, and 
then came up triumphantly sitting 
on her rump. . 

His first triumph was short-lived, 
for the net collapsed and. man and 
beast plunged into the water again. 
The second effort was successful ond, 
once on Jand, the cow became sud- 
denly quict. 

She gave no more trouble and soon, 
In that disinterested woy cows have, 
was unconcernedly chewing the cud.

would agree to peace. They 
are: : ;

dependence of Spain and 

freedom from all ‘outside 
intcrforonce.

by moans of popular and 
secret clection, to be allow- 
ed to choose their own 
-form of Government.-- - > 
3.—No reprisals of any 
naturo aftor the conclusion 
of. peace. 

The rest of Dr. Negrin's 
speech was of a .-fighting 
nature, in which he declared 
that the Loyalists would still 
triumph,

across the Siu River, where

attack since December.

urtiiery barrage from the Japanese,

construcUon of pontoon — bridges,

effect .a crorsing. ;

Hunan, and not even sporadic fight! 


Ing is reported. .

Ishth, :

area,

Mr. Rublee For London 


. ’ Berlin, Feb. 2. 

Mr, George Rublee is leaving for 
London to-night... ar ae 

A- communique states that the talks 
proceeded.on the basis of the surres- 
dons for ww programme of orderly 
emigration of Jews made by Dr. 
Schacht during his visit to London: 

It udds that the conversations 
clarified the programme, so that Mr, 
Rublee Is able to give details to the

plan has been modified beyond re- 
cognition, and it Is understood that 
there Is now no‘ question of an out- 
side loan, nor additlona! German

~ Gold ‘Shares 
Stimulated 
By New Bill . 


Effect Of British Govt. 
Announcement 


ahs LONDON, Feb. 2. 
GOLD MINING shares rose 


on the Stock Exchange to-day | 


following the issue Jast night of 
the Government’s Bill amending 
the currency and Bank Notes 
Acts, as a result of which the 
gold holding of the Bank of 
England is to be revalued at: 
market price, 


The step was foreseen at the time], 


of the Treasury onnouncement of 
the fiduciary issue carly last month, 
but other proposals contained in the 
BI have taken the publle by sur- 
prise, and they are generally re- 
farded us constituting an important 
stauyze in the development of the 
British. monetary system. 

The Bill provides that the Bank’s 
gold reserve and other rsscts of the 
Issue department will be subject to 
revaluntion weeldy ut current prices, 
tnd that any difference between this 
valuatlon and note circulation shall 
be adjusted by movements of gold 
between the Bank and the Exchange 
Equatisation Account. 

The Bilt will also reduce the 
fiduciary jssue so recently increased 
to £400,000,000 to £300,000,000 und 
will repenl oa section of the 1644 
Bank Charter Act which obliges the 
Bank to {issue notes-in return for 
gold, oat 

It is slated that there is no preee- 
dent in central banking history for 
the proposal that the gold reserve 
be valued at the Independent market 
price, The object ci the measure is 
assumed to be to introduce greatcr 
flexibility Into the “management” of 
the nationol | monetary system, of 
which the British authoritles | now 


have very considerable experience, 
and the comment of financial writers 
{s generally. . f{avourable.—British 
Wireless.

CONSTRUCTION OF © 
~~ BRECONSHIRE — 
A BRILLIANT -FEAT 


HIPPING NEWSPAPERS throughout the world this 
morning published a brief paragraph from Lloyd’s of 


London. ¥ 


It comprised only half ‘x dozen words and tersely said: 
“HONGKONG.—Launched, Breconshire, 10,000-tons, 


Glen Line.” 


Behind that brief message, one of hundreds received 
annually at the famous Shipping headquarters in London 
and distributed by’ them throughout the world, is. the. 
story of the third largest vessel ‘built in the British 
Empire outside the United Kingdom. 


It §s the story of the Inbour 
of a thousand men, of the skill 
of British engineers and de- 
signers, and of the triumph of 
Hongkong ship-building yards. 

“The Breconshire is the first of two 
10,000-ton Eners to be launched in 
Hongkong this year. Work on the 
second, ulso under construction at the 
Talkoo Dockyards, $s alrendy well 
cadvanced, 
The gathering gloom-of nightfal! 
ndded to the splendid scene at the 
Talkoo Docks yesterday, when the 
Inunching took place, 

Outside the docks, powerful tugs 
directed thelr searchlights on the 
towering ship waiting to take charge 
of her, in’ the puffing and important 
way tugs have, after she slid into 
the water. 

Beneath the hull, us the time for 
the Inunching approached, gangs of 
‘workmen were bus!ly engaged knock- 
ing away- the blocks, leaving only 
four giant iron triggers to hold the 
vessel on the slipway unt!) the wife of 
Wis Excellency the Governor pressed 
an electric. button. 

The preliminaries were watched 
with ypreat interest by the large 
crowd of over a thousand suests ine 
vited to attend the ceremonies. 


UNIQUE CEREMONY 


The ceremony was unique in many 
respects. It was the first launching 
to be performed by Lady, Northcote; 
it took place in the re-created day- 
light of hundreds of flood-lights in 
the presence of the largest attend- 
fnce ever witnessed at a: function of 
this nature; and it was the first such 
event to be brondcast by ZBW. . 

His Excellency the Governor, Sir 
Geaflry Northcote, was also present. 
He was accompanied by his son, Mr. 
M. A, Northcote, who arrived in the 
Colony yesterday. and was attended 
by his A.D.C., Capt, S. H. Batty- 
Smith and his Private Secretary, Mr. 
Garrod Treverton. 

His Excellency and Lady North- 
vote were recelved by Mr. W. H. 
Lock, manager of Messrs, Butter- 
ficld and Swire, and Mr. J. Finnie, 


manager of the Taikoo Dockyard. 


Also present on the platform were 
Major-General A. E. and Mrs, 
Grasett, Sir Atholl and Lady. Mac- 
Gregor, Commodore and Mrs. E. B. 
C, Dicken, the Hon. Mr. N. L. and 
Mrs. Smith,’ the Hon. -Mr. C. G. 
Alabaster, -the-Hon;-Mr, J; J;-Pater- 
son, the Hon. Sir Shouson Chow, 
the Hon, Mr. 'S. H. and Mrs, Dod- 
well, Sir Vandeleur Grayburn and 
Miss Grayburn, Mr. and Mrs. E. Cock, 
Mr. and Mrs, C. C, Roberts, Mr. and 
Mrs. D, L. Newblizging and Mr. and 
Mrs, A. R. H. Phillips, 


BOUQUET PRESENTED 


Before the christening ceremony, 
Lady Northcote was presented with 
a beauti(ul bouquet of roses by little 
Miss Moyra Macindoe, daughter of 
Mr. A. MucIndoe, of Talkoo Docks. 

The giant mallets wielded by the 
workmen as they knocked away the 
blocks lent an almost weird emphasis 
to the scene ‘ns Lady Northcote, 

1 


bathed in the light of powerful Mood- 
lamps, mounted the dals, 

Lady Northcote's voice was relayed 
by amplifiers as she quietly gave the 
ship the name that was subsequently 
Mashed by cable to all parts of the 
world an the Iatest notable oddition 
to the British Mercantile ficet. 


SHIP NAMED 


“I name you Breconshire, and [ 
wish.God specd to you and all 
yours,” Lady Northcote said, 

The wife of the Governor then 
grasped the bottle of chnomparne 
swinging from the bow of the ship. 

Simultancously, “the  searchligh 
flashed on to the path along which 
the newly-christened vessel would 
slip Into her natural element. 


The towcring muss started to move 
instantancously with the bursting of 
the bottle of champagne against her 
port bow. 


Amids vociferous cheers and, the 
time-honoured firing of crackers, she 
moved slowly down the stocks and 
slid gracefully into the water. 

To the cheering spectators, the ten 
thousand tons of stecl moulded into 
the shapely lines of a> new ship, 
seemed to disappear inio the night, 
until all that remnined to identify her 
was 4 cluster of twinkling lMghts, 

All around the world, listeners with 
radio sels werg tuned {into the vivid 
brondcast of the ceremony from ZBW. 
The noise of the crackers and of 
cheering crowds gave lLsteners in 


. Australia, in Canada, in South Africa 
tandsin other distant 


Jands where 
ZBW is known, an. almost televisory 
Picture of the launching of Hong- 
Isong's largest ner. ~ : 

The guests subsequently - repaired 
to the spatious moulding loft, gaily 
decorated for the occasion, where a 
toast to the success of the vessel was 
honoured with enthusiasm,

fram the party in consequence of his

will shortly leave Hanoi for Europe.

cently applicd to the Ministry of 
Forcign Affairs, has already been re- 
leased from Chungking, it is jearned.

Visit To Macao 


Macno, Feb, 2. 

Under the command of Licut.- 
Comm. D. L. C, Craig, H.M.S, Sea- 
mew arrived in Macao on a short 
visit yesterday afternoon, The usual 
exchange of salutes and official calls 
were given when the Seamew berthed 
In Portuguese waters. 

The Scamew’s officers were the 
guests of Mr. F. J. Gellion, British 
Vice-Consul for Macao, und Mrs, 


-Gellion, for dinner yes\erday evening, 


and o luncheon was held in thelr 
honour by ILE, the Governor of 
Macao, Dr. A. Tamagnini Barbosa, 
to-day. The Governor and his offi- 
cial party dined on board the Sea- 
mew this evening and an At Home 
was held shortly before noon on the 
visiting warship. —~ : 

The ship's company engaged a local 
team in a soccer match this afternoon 
nnd the Scamew's team lost by a 
score of three goals to two. 

1LM.S, Seamew will Jeave this port 
to-morrow morning.—Our Own Cor- 
respondent. ‘ :

PRESS CRITICISM 


New York, Feb. 2. 

The secrecy surrounding the forelgn 
policy of the United States is gencr- 
ally deplored in press comments on 
President Roosevelt’s reported state- 
mont. 3 : 

The “Herald-Tribune’ says that 
while President Roosevelt's foreign 
policies." have been as admirable ns 
they are bold, nevertheless, when the 
issue is as grave as peace and war In 
the balance, the right for the pcople 
to be informed seems paramount. 

The “Chicago Tribune” says that 
the White House has become an tm- 
perial palace. President Roosevelt is 
acting as Jf he were absolute ruler of 
a people who have-nothing to say 
about their own destiny, and who 
could be sold into war as lightly as If 
they were 18th century Hessians. 

The “Washington Post’ declares 
thdt: the Administration's efforts to 
facilitate the sale of planes to France 
is a possible factor in the slight re- 


| laxation of tension noted in Europe.— 


peace. Reuter, 
REPUBLICANS ACT 


» Washington, Feb, 2. 
‘Nine Republican members of the 
House of Representatives Forcign 
Affairs Committce to-day {ssucd a 
statement In which they “deplore and 
protest against the un-neutral actions 
of the secret methods of the Presi- 
dent” in connection with the sale of 
aeroplanes to France. 

There is no objection ogainst the 
sule of acroplanes to a. nation to 
which the United States Is diploma- 
Ucally related, “but we insist that 
such un-neutral acts entangle us in 
forelgn conflicts, and endanger the 
peace of America.” . 

They appealed to the President to 
present the facts to the American 
people who “irrespective of party, are 
i:elng committed to a war programme 
through secret diplomacy.” 

The statement urged the President 
fo uphold traditlonnl forelyn policy 
of neutrality, non-intervention, and 
pence.—Reuter,

GERMANY’S 
U-BOAT 
REARMAMENT 
‘Official 
Communique Issued 


In Berlin 


BERLIN, Feb. 2. 

THE RECENT Anglo-German 
naval conference in Berlin, in 
the course of which Germany 
unnounced her intention of in- 
creasing her submarine tonnage 
up to the limit provided for in 
the Anglo-German naval agree- 
ment, was the subject of the fol- 
lowing announcement made. by 
the semi-oflicinl German News 
Agency this evening: 

“The German Government inform~ 
ed His Britannic. Majesty's Govern- 
ment Jast December of its intention 
of exercising the rights accorded to 
it through the Anglo-German naval 


agreement of June 18, 1934, and July 
17, 1937, 


“A friendly conference was held 
in Berlin on December 30, concerning 
certain, questions that arose from the 
exercising of. these rights. 


“The German Government, agreed 
ut that time to provide the British 
Government with a written states 
ment, 


TO BEGIN IMMEDIATELY 


“On January 18 of this year the 
German Government handed to the 
British Government n written state- 
ment regarding the intentions as ree 
vealed at the nforementloned confer- 
ence. : 


“Germany will, accordingly, in- 
crease her submarine tonnage to 
purity with that of the British Em- 
pire, the beginning to be made in 
1939, and construction to be con- 
tinued until the treaty limit. fs 
achieved, 


“Germany will, furihermore, arm 
two 10,000-ton criigerg In such a 
manner that they will be advanced 
from cruisers of sub-class B to 
crulsers of sub-class A, In doing 50, 
Germany Is. exercising the right 
aecorded to her in the agreement.”— 
Trans-Ocean,

FOR N.B.C. 


NETWORK _ 


FOR THE SECOND time in history, special programmes 


broadcast from ZBW have been 
Stated. 


In response to a request from the 

Natlonal Broadcasting Corporation of 
America, Hongkong’s broadcasting 
station went on the alr at 9 o'clock 
yesterday morning. 
“ Only ‘the short-wave transmitter 
was used, the programme bcing 
picked up in Manila, and relayed 
from there by_ trans-Pacifle ‘radlo- 
phone to San Francisco. 


MILLIONS HEAR 


From San Francisco, ZBW's special 
programme was broadcast through 
the N.B.C, network of statlond to 
millions of American Isteners, 

The broadeast was made in con- 
nection with the Tenth Anniversary 
Programme of the First International 
Brondcast by the N.B.C, 

The first international broadcast 
was a relay, in 1920, of an orchestral 
concert from London to the United 
States over the N.B.C. network. 

The singer from ZBDW yesterday 
morning was Miss Law Shun-hing, 
who has also sung in both San 
Francisco and Honolulu. Typical 
Cantonese music was also relayed 
from Hongkong to American listeners, 

Messages from the United States 
yesterday afternoon indicated that 
reception of ZBW's programme was 


iM . 

In additlon to Hongkong, the 
N.B.C, relayed special programmes 
from London, Tokyo, Manila ond 
Honolulu.

5,000 GUERILLAS NEAR 


- According to “Trans-Ocean," there 
are 5,000 Chinese guerillas in and 
around Kuling, and the Japanese 
military authorities have declded to 
try and eliminate them in the near 
future. Because of this, and the con- 
sequent dunger, they have requested 


the foreigners to evacuate forthwith, 
giving them, as yet, only ten days in 
which to carry out the request, 


ROUTE MADE. KNOWN 


‘The Japanese demand specifies that 
the foreigners must evacuate before 
next Friday. The route, — the 
Japanese further direct, should be 
taken from Lushan through Lien- 
hwatung to Kiuldang.

nationals must act in a group and, by

methods, show that they are not com- 
batants, 

After descending the mountain, the 
foreiyners must obey the directlons 
of the commander of the Japanesa 
troops stationed in Kiukiang, whether

other places, ;

KING ACTS. 


IN CRISIS 
IN BELGIUM 


4 BRUSSELS, Feb. 2. 

_ KING LEOPOLD'S interven- 
tion in certain questions of home 
policy in the course of the 
Cabinet Council meeting held 
here to-day was stressed in a 
communique issued later by 
Premier Spank. «| 

The communtquc states that the 
King dwelt on the necessity firstly, 
of precision in matters of economic 
policy, secondly, of ndaption of a 
budget to the financial resources of 
the country, and thirdly, of the 
development of social Iegislatlon. 

The King also discussed the ques- 
tion of constitutional guarantees, and 
emphasised the principle of separa- 
tion of executive -and legislative 
powers, and insisted that odministra- 
tive reforms should be carried out 
without further delay. 

Informed quarters add that the 
king expressed the earnest desire 
that the constantly Increasing fric- 
tion between the parties in Parlia- 
ment should be diminished in the 
interests of the natlon.—Trans-Occan,

LOYALIST APPEAL 
DISALLOWED 


The House of Lords, as the 
supreme court of appellate judgment, 
to-day disallowed the appeal lodged 
by the Spanish Loyalist Government 
agalnst the judgment of the Court of 
Appeal, confirming the arrest of the 
Spanish steamer Arantzazu Mendl, in 
British waters. 

The ‘steamer, which belonged to a 
Spanish Insurgent owner had been 
requisitioned by the Loyalist 
Governme’t, claiming to be the only 
legnl authority [In Spain, whereupon, 
at the demand of the owner, she was 
plnced “under arrest’ {n a British 
port. i 

The judgment of: the - House- of 
Lords {s important since, following 
the previous decision of the. Court ot 
Appeal, it expressly recognises the 
Insurgent Government as the Gov~- 
ernment of o sovereign, independent 
State.—Trans-Occan.

INSURGENTS CONTINUE 
: ‘ADVANCE 


Replying to 


Burgos, Feb. 2. 
Insurgent troops to-day occupied 
Berga in the valley of Llobregat. 


Loyalist troops are reported to be 
retreating further on all fronts in the 
direction of the French frontier, 

A detalled statement regarding the 
capture of Berga says that the Insur- 
gents advanced on Thursday morning 
in two columns on the town, com- 
pletely surrounding the Loyalist 
garrison, which soon ceased to offer 
further resistance, 


The occupation of Berga brings the |: 


Insurgent troops in this sector to 
within 35 miles of the French frontier 
at Pulgeerda. + 

It js further onnounced that the 
Maestrazgo army corps has advanced 
a considerable distance oeyond Vich 
and has captured the villages of Seva, 
Taradell, and Ruldeperas, thus cn- 
veloping the Mont Seny mountain 
range in which the Loyalist artillery 
is entrenched.—Trans-Occan.

PHE MOMENTOUS news that 
President Roosevelt has 
pledged American support to 
democracy in the cause of a war 
with the Totalitarian countries 
should have a sobering | in- 
fluence upon those who favour 
the use of force in. international 
affairs. ; 

It is quite. understandable 
that America should announce 
her policy, for ‘as i great de- 
mocracy, she naturally feels it 
her duty to do all in her power 
to assist other democratic coun- 
tries which may be threatercd. 

The freedom of the subject 
is the very corner-stone of de-! 
mocratic principles, and any 
movement which threatens to) 
challenge that freedom must be 
vigorously opposed. 

The — wave of nationalism. 
which has swept through Ger- 
many and Italy has crented a 
danger which cannot be vver- 
looked. Nationalism in itself is 
an admirable quality, but when 
it becomes so revolutionary in 
nature as to constitute a men- 
ace to others, the others, may 
rightly be expected to take 
every possible safeguard in the 
Interest of self-preservation. 

If Germany considers that 
she has been singled out as the 
main threat to democracy, rhe 
has only herself to blame. The 


public utterances made by her 
masters have had anything but 
a soothing effect on the rest of 
the world, and it naturally 
follows that when danger is 
supposed to exist, it is only 
logical and sensible that those 
likely to be affected should or- 


ganise united action for resis- 


tance. 
The United States - will do 


everything possible to keep out 
of war, and rightly so. 
can Statesmen have followed 
events 
apprehension, and there can be 
no doubt that from the Presi- 
dent downwards, they are fully 
aware of the policies which are 
at ‘the root of the trouble in 
Europe to-day. :

A COMPREHENSIVE SCHEME for 


Civilian Shadow Pian 
For Great Britain 


LONDON, Feb. 2. 
the organisation of civil 


defence, involving the provisional appointment of regional 
political governors, is announced by Sir John Anderson, _ 


The pian is practically complete and 
will exist in shadow form in peace- 
time, ready to operate Immediately an 
emergency arises. 

A Repienal Commissioner, ‘a man 
of natlonal understanding, capable of 
undertaking the great responsibility” 
will be appointed in cach of ten 
British regions, and one ench for 
Wales and Scotland. 

Tn the event of a region becoming: 
shut off from the centre of Govern- 
ment, the Commissioners will exercise 
the full authority of the Gove 
ment, 

There will be an effective link be- 
tween the civil and = military au- 
Uhorilies, and the powers of the Com- 
missioners will not extend to military 
matter 

The scheme also provided devolu- 
tion In the A.R.P, organisation by the 
creation of 38° regional oMcers to 
enable treatment of local problems on 
the spot, and to lessen the need for 
appeal to the central department. 

The Regional Commissioners wll 
be provided with a war staff, constst- 
ing of A.R.P. regional officers and 
representatives of all Government de- 
partments concerned with civil de- 
fence, like health, transport, and food. 

The Commissioners in’ peace-time 
will maintain frequent contact with 
representatives of all the relevant 
Government departments. This, in 
-effect, umounts to peace-time meet- 
jings of the war staff. : 

The degree of control exercised by 
the Commissioners in war-time would 
vary according to the extent to which 
communications were affected, but in 
case of need, the region twill function 
as self-contained —Reuter, : 


DEFENCE PROGRESS 


London, Feb. 2. 

The Prime Minister, in a statement 
dn the House of Commons to-day said 
that the Re-Armament Advisory 
Panel of business men had reported 
that the service departments support- 
ed by the Treasury were rapidly ac- 
complishin: a most diMeult ‘task of 
Rreat complexity with eMiciency,

TWO GIRLS 
JOIN JUNK 
ADVENTURE 


Voyage Across The 
Pacific To US. — 


SHANGHAI, Feb, 2. 

Two GOOD-LOOKING Russian 

girls, who were formerly 
cocktail bar hostesses, Lubna 
Schennikoff uged 23, and Olga 
Alksne, aged 22, are accompany- 
ing a crew of eight men on a 
round-the-world Chinese junk 
trip aboard the “Adventure,” 
which left for Manila yesterday 
morning. 


The junk is the one which started 
from Shanghai on December 30, but 
which was forced to return when 
pirates riddled the: drinking water 
tank with bullets, 


The crew is practically the same, 
consisting; of Captain Rakieh, a 
Czecho-Slovaizian, Leo Tomastevsky, 
a Russian-Freneh policeman and 
former Shanghai sprinting champion, 
Leo - Rudchenka,. another Russian- 
French.. policeman.-and former--440 
yards running champion, Diatalcoft, 
Vinokuroff, and Komar, Russians, 
Dzull # Latvian, and Kireeff, on Tartar. 

The “Adventure” is going first to 
Manila, and thence expects to sall to 
the Celebes, Java, Dutch East Indies, 
and ports cn route to New Zealand, 
from where the junit will go across 
to San Francisco. It also plans to 
go through the Panama, homeward 
bound via the Mediterranean. It: js 
expected that the trip will lust 18 
months.—-United Press, ;

The Last Trip 


HE last time the Philippincs com- 
peted in Davis Cup play was in 
1928 with the Aragon _ brothers, 
Guillermo and Francisco, who were 
then at the. height of their careers, 
as representatives. The Aragon 
brothers met the American players 
and lost five matches to nil. Lope 
Yngayo and Guillermo Aragon re- 
presented the Islands in 1922. They 
journeyed to’ Vienna “where «they 
played against the Austrians and lost 
four matches to one. This year the 
Phillppine team will be selected from 
a field of young players which in- 
cludes Amado Sanchez, No, 1 rank- 
Ing player. Felicisime Ampon, No. 3 
ranking ace,, and Juan Ladaw. 
Leonardo Gavia,.also at No. 1 posi- 
Hon this season, Ja the only veteran 
of the lot who will try out. for the 
trip. The trials will be held before 
the month of May. Both Sanchez 
and Gavia were seen in action here

Schmeling Denies 
Trouble With The 
German G 


overnment 


: New York, Feb. 2. 
heavyweight boxer, arrived here 
stain of his one-round knock-out 


by Joe Louis, the present champion. 


He denled he had been in trouble 
with ‘the German Government, and 
denied reports that he had threatencd 
to “break Goebbels’ neck" if the 
German Propngandist Minister 
molested his wife, Anny Ondra, He 
sald he, last saw Goebbels about six 
weeks ago at a dinner. 


In response . to n question as to 
whether he had been 
United States to prove ho was not in 
a concentration camp, he said “If I 
were in a_ concentration camp, 
would not be here." He sald he 
had planned to confer with Mike 
Jacobs, the promoter, to-day. 


The German boxer says he Is 
willing to fight anybody. “I plan to 
stay here from elght to 14 days,. but 
longer if a fight js arranged. 
would sure Ilke to fight Louis 
again, I still think I can beat bim,” 
he added.—Unlted Preas. '

RECORD 
EQUALLED 


Rain Kept Him Waiting 
For Two Days 


“London, Jan, 19. 

Don Bridman, after rain hnd kept 
him waiting for two days, completed 
his century for South Australia 
ayuinst New South Wales on 18th 
January, and equalled C. B. Fry's 
first-class cricket record by scoring 
centuries in six successive innings. 

Bradman, 22 not out on Saturday, 
carried his score to 135 not out, and 
d South Australia’s inn- 
ings closed at 349 for four, giving 
them ao first innings lead of 103. 

In their second Innings New South 
Wales scored 156 for five wickets, 
and the match was left diniwn, 

Sinee -he returned from the tour 
in England, which ended for him 
with a-foot injury in the last Test, 
Bradman has played only six first- 
class innings. In these he has scor- 
ed 914 runs for an’ average of 182.8.

OUR GUIDE 
TO THE 
CINEMAS 


“South Riding” (King’s Theatre, to- 
day)—Rural Yorkshire comes to the 
screen in this picture produced by 
London Pavillon. Edna Best Ralph 
Richardson, Edmund Gwenn and Ann 
Todd take the chief roles. Carefully 
and sincerely madc—good drama. 

“Sixty Glorlous Years" (Queen's 
and Alhambra Theatres, to-day).—A. 
great historic pageant jn technicolour, 
with Anna Neagle ogain as Queen 
Victoria. The cast includes Anton 
Walbrook, C, Aubrey Smith and Olat 
Olsen. Herbert Wilcox has again 
made n good job of the direction, pre- 
senting all the significant events 
during the long and glorious relgm of 
Queen Victorja, 

“Three -Comrades’* (Oriental 
Theatre, to-day).—A strangely mov- 
ing story of three comrades after the 
war and thelr reactions to peace- 
time Mfe immediately after 1918, 
Robert Taylor, Franchot Tone, 
Robert Young and Margaret Sullavan. 

“Judge Mardy's Children” (Majes- 
tle Theatre, to-day)—Another one 
of the popular series. When Judge 
Hardy gets oa job at Washington, 
lobbyists drag the family into o plot 
that looks Ike ruin and disgrace 
until the Judge steps in and turna 
the tables. .

Visit To Tientsin

CHUNGKING, Feb. 8. 
‘ SIR ARCHIBALD KERR 
CLARK KERR, the British Am- 
bassador to China, 
accorded a hearty welcome by 
the Chinese people in Tientsin 
since his arrival there. 


During the past two days, accord- 
ing to a Tientsin message received 
here, a large number of ietters havo 
been ‘sent to the Brillsh envoy by the 
Chinese people, paying respects and 
declaring that they are loyal to the 
National Government and will sup- 
port the present war of resistance to 
the bitter end. 


Representative Louls Johnson and 
members of the appropriations sub- 
committee attended, and one official 
infon that the mect- 
as_comprechensive 
os lost Tucnday's secret gathering of 


mittce.—Reuter,

From A Tin Of 
Salmon 


TIN of satmon Is a good stand-by 

to have in the larder for the un- 
expected pucst—but -how many 
people serve thelr tinned salmon 
10 


Try it in white sauce:—Open ao tin 
of salmon,~and strain off the juice. 
Mnke an ordinary white sauce with 
butter, flour, apd milk—but in- 
corporate the salmon Jjulce in the 
Don't forget to season well. 
Put the salmon (broken up) Into the 
sauce to heat, 


sauce, 


While -it [s heating make four 
small slices of toast. Dish the sal- 
mon mixture on this, and garnish 
with chopped hard-bolled egg, and 
parsley on top, | 


Salmon a UItallan 


Seald, peel, and sllee-three toma- 
tors. Fry one minced onion in a 
little fat and Jay on a greased fire- 
pesot dish... Add half of the tomn- 

cs. 


Open a tin of salmon. Floke it up 
and lay on top of tomatoes. Season 
well. Put on remainder of tomatoes, 
and sprinkle weil with white bread- 
crumbs. 


Mix together the ojl from the 
tinned salmon, tho fat in which the 
onions were fried, and a tnblicspoon 
of water. Pour this.over the mix- 
ture, and bake in a moderate hot 
oven 20 minutes. . 


Sulmon Loaf 


Flake up the contents of a tin of 
salmon. MIx In wlth it 2 oz. bread- 
crumbs, grated rind of 44 lemon, 1 
fenspdon minced onion, 2 eggs well 
beaten, 1 gill milk, and seasonings of 
Prpper and salt. 

Pour all into a greased bread tin. 
Bake in a mederate oven 29-30 
minutes. Turn out, and garnish with 
slices of lemon. Pour % pint white 
sauce round. = == 

Try using tinned salmon with 
mashed potatoes for a fish ple; or 
combine it with rice, (cooked) for 
kedgeree. You will find the result 
is much tastier than using ordinary 
white fish.

__ Fined For 
Dog Offences 


Lt. R. L. Boddy residing at Princo 
Edward Road, Kowloon, was fined 
$5 by Mr. EB, Himsworth at the Kow~ 
toon’ Mngistracy this morning when 
he admitted a summons of allowing o 
whito chow dog astray without a 
lend or muzzie on January 12. 

Defendant claimed that he was un- 
aware that hls dog had left the house 
without lead or muzzle until he 
recelved the summons, It. was quite 
possible that the dog had one out 
on its own by way of the garden gate. 
The dog, nid Lt. Boddy, was only 
a puppy, aged four months. . 

In imposing the Onc, the magis- 
trate told defendant that he should 
be more cautious, 


DOG'S “PERIOD OF WILDNESS” 


Mr, J. M, Jacks was fined SB for 
altowing his brown and white dog 
out in Cumberland Road at 6.30 on, 
January’ 32, when he admitted the 
offence before Mr. E, Himsworth at 
the Kowloon Magistracy this morn- 
ing. 

Defendant claimed that he was 
unaware that his dog had gone out 
on the day in question, He received 
the summons, and this was the first 
thne that he knew of the affair. 
Defendant further stated that he did 
not possess a “brown and white dos” 
as stated in his summony., His dox, 
2 polnter, had a brown face, but was 
“definitely a black and white: dog." 

Mr, Jacks stated that his dog had 
been having a “period of wildness 
for over a week and ft was possible, 
that the don hod gone out on tts own,

Pressure On Japan:

Mr. Arita, Foreign Minister, stated 
to-day that it js too much to assume 
that cconomic pressure ayy Japan 
has been preelpitately increased, 

He denied that British and Ameri- 
can assistance to Chiang Kai-shek 
wos dirceted against Japan, . 

Tho Forcitn Minister reiterated 
that Japan does not intend to close 
the open door to Third Powers, He 
sald the establishment of a new 
order was to enable all people «to 
assume thelr proper place.—United

Air Service 
: Wasiington, Feb, 2. 
> Imperial Airways hope to begin a 
scheduled seasonal service of an 
experimental nature between 
Southampton antl New _ York on 
June 1, according to the British note 
published by the State Department. 
The route will be from Southamp- 
ton to Shannon, Botwood (New- 
foundlund),. Montreal, -and New 
York. 
The flying boats will probably leave 


‘once a weels jn each direction, later 
increasing to twice a weeks. 


{s believed American companics may 
be able to start a service between 
March 15 and March 31.—Reuter.
